288	\def\@normalsize{\@setsize\normalsize{11pt}\xpt\@xpt}
289	\def\small{\@setsize\small{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt}
290	\def\footnotesize{\@setsize\footnotesize{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt}
291	\def\scriptsize{\@setsize\scriptsize{8pt}\viipt\@viipt}
292	\def\tiny{\@setsize\tiny{7pt}\vipt\@vipt}
293	\def\large{\@setsize\large{14pt}\xiipt\@xiipt}
294	\def\Large{\@setsize\Large{16pt}\xivpt\@xivpt}
295	\def\LARGE{\@setsize\LARGE{20pt}\xviipt\@xviipt}
296	\def\huge{\@setsize\huge{23pt}\xxpt\@xxpt}
297	\def\Huge{\@setsize\Huge{28pt}\xxvpt\@xxvpt}

302	  \RequirePackage{hyperref}
303	  % make links dark blue
304	  \definecolor{darkblue}{rgb}{0, 0, 0.5}
305	  \hypersetup{colorlinks=true, citecolor=darkblue, linkcolor=darkblue, urlcolor=darkblue}
306	\else

308	  % It provides a backup, no-op definiton of \href.
309	  % This is necessary because \href command is used in the acl_natbib.bst file.
310	  \def\href#1#2{{#2}}
311	  \usepackage{url}
312	\fi

513	% bibinfo.check avoids acting on missing fields while bibinfo.warn will
514	% issue a warning message if a missing field is detected. Prior to calling
515	% the bibinfo functions, the user should push the field value and then its
516	% name string, in that order.
517	FUNCTION {bibinfo.check}
518	{ swap$
519	  duplicate$ missing$
520	    {
521	      pop$ pop$
522	      ""
523	    }
524	    { duplicate$ empty$
525	        {
526	          swap$ pop$
527	        }
528	        { swap$
529	          pop$
530	        }
531	      if$
532	    }
533	  if$
534	}
535	FUNCTION {bibinfo.warn}
536	{ swap$
537	  duplicate$ missing$
538	    {
539	      swap$ "missing " swap$ * " in " * cite$ * warning$ pop$
540	      ""
541	    }
542	    { duplicate$ empty$
543	        {
544	          swap$ "empty " swap$ * " in " * cite$ * warning$
545	        }
546	        { swap$
547	          pop$
548	        }
549	      if$
550	    }
551	  if$
552	}
553	INTEGERS { nameptr namesleft numnames }
554	
555	
556	STRINGS  { bibinfo}
557	
558	FUNCTION {format.names}
559	{ 'bibinfo :=
560	  duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$ {
561	  's :=
562	  "" 't :=
563	  #1 'nameptr :=
564	  s num.names$ 'numnames :=
565	  numnames 'namesleft :=
566	    { namesleft #0 > }
567	    { s nameptr
568	      "{ff~}{vv~}{ll}{, jj}" % first name first for all authors
569	      format.name$
570	      bibinfo bibinfo.check
571	      't :=
572	      nameptr #1 >
573	        {
574	          nameptr #19	% truncate after 19 names
575	          #1 + =
576	          numnames #20	% if there are more than 20 names
577	          > and
578	            { "others" 't :=
579	              #1 'namesleft := }
580	            'skip$
581	          if$		% end truncation of long list of names
582	          namesleft #1 >
583	            { ", " * t * }
584	            {
585	              s nameptr "{ll}" format.name$ duplicate$ "others" =
586	                { 't := }
587	                { pop$ }
588	              if$
589	              numnames #2 >
590	                { "," * }
591	                'skip$
592	              if$
593	              t "others" =
594	                {
595			  %%                 " " * bbl.etal *
596			  % compute the number of remaining authors
597			  " and " * numnames nameptr - #1 + int.to.str$ * " others" *
598	                }
599	                {
600	                  bbl.and
601	                  space.word * t *
602	                }
603	              if$
604	            }
605	          if$
606	        }
607	        't
608	      if$
609	      nameptr #1 + 'nameptr :=
610	      namesleft #1 - 'namesleft :=
611	    }
612	  while$
613	  } if$
614	}
615	FUNCTION {format.names.ed}
616	{
617	  format.names
618	}

652	        if$

668	FUNCTION {format.full.names}
669	{'s :=
670	 "" 't :=
671	  #1 'nameptr :=
672	  s num.names$ 'numnames :=
673	  numnames 'namesleft :=
674	    { namesleft #0 > }
675	    { s nameptr
676	      "{vv~}{ll}" format.name$
677	      't :=
678	      nameptr #1 >
679	        {
680	          namesleft #1 >
681	            { ", " * t * }
682	            {
683	              s nameptr "{ll}" format.name$ duplicate$ "others" =
684	                { 't := }
685	                { pop$ }
686	              if$
687	              t "others" =
688	                {
689	                  " " * bbl.etal *
690	                }
691	                {
692	                  numnames #2 >
693	                    { "," * }
694	                    'skip$
695	                  if$
696	                  bbl.and
697	                  space.word * t *
698	                }
699	              if$
700	            }
701	          if$
702	        }
703	        't
704	      if$
705	      nameptr #1 + 'nameptr :=
706	      namesleft #1 - 'namesleft :=
707	    }
708	  while$
709	}

761	FUNCTION {output.bibitem.original} % urlbst (renamed from output.bibitem, so it can be wrapped below)
762	{ newline$
763	  "\bibitem[{" write$
764	  label write$
765	  ")" make.full.names duplicate$ short.list =
766	     { pop$ }
767	     { * }
768	   if$
769	  "}]{" * write$
770	  cite$ write$
771	  "}" write$
772	  newline$
773	  ""
774	  before.all 'output.state :=
775	}

777	FUNCTION {n.dashify}
778	{
779	  't :=
780	  ""
781	    { t empty$ not }
782	    { t #1 #1 substring$ "-" =
783	        { t #1 #2 substring$ "--" = not
784	            { "--" *
785	              t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=
786	            }
787	            {   { t #1 #1 substring$ "-" = }
788	                { "-" *
789	                  t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=
790	                }
791	              while$
792	            }
793	          if$
794	        }
795	        { t #1 #1 substring$ *
796	          t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=
797	        }
798	      if$
799	    }
800	  while$
801	}

839	          emphasize * }

842	    }

853	                { bbl.number capitalize }
854	              if$

856	              bbl.in space.word *

879	INTEGERS { multiresult }
880	FUNCTION {multi.page.check}
881	{ 't :=
882	  #0 'multiresult :=
883	    { multiresult not
884	      t empty$ not
885	      and
886	    }
887	    { t #1 #1 substring$
888	      duplicate$ "-" =
889	      swap$ duplicate$ "," =
890	      swap$ "+" =
891	      or or
892	        { #1 'multiresult := }
893	        { t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't := }
894	      if$
895	    }
896	  while$
897	  multiresult
898	}

908	        }
909	      if$
910	      tie.or.space.prefix

912	      * *
913	    }
914	  if$
915	}

1099	FUNCTION {archiveprefix.or.eprinttype} % holder for eprinttype with archiveprefix precedence
1100	{
1101	  archiveprefix empty$
1102	  {
1103	    eprinttype empty$
1104	      { "" } % not using 'skip$ to reduce errors like "nothing to pop from stack"
1105	      { eprinttype }
1106	    if$
1107	  }
1108	  { archiveprefix }
1109	  if$
1110	}
1111	
1112	FUNCTION {output.eprint} % this is only used with the @misc record type (common for arXiv and other preprint server bibtex records)
1113	{
1114	  eprint empty$
1115	    {% if eprint field is empty
1116	      publisher field.or.null "arXiv" = % field.or.null here helps when no publisher field in the record
1117	        { publisher " preprint" * } % add " preprint" to publisher with the idea that publisher is the name of the preprint server
1118	        { "" } % if publisher != "arXiv" then empty output
1119	      if$
1120	      emphasize % no output function after emphasize because nothing goes after this
1121	    }
1122	    {% if eprint field is not empty
1123	      archiveprefix.or.eprinttype empty$
1124	        { "" } % not using 'skip$ to reduce errors like "nothing to pop from stack"
1125	        {% if archiveprefix or eprinttype fields are not empty
1126	          journal empty$
1127	            { "Preprint" } % if journal field is empty: output just "Preprint" emphasized like a journal name
1128	            { journal } % if journal field is not empty, output it (takes precedence)
1129	          if$
1130	          emphasize output % emphasize what we formed before, setting output as a border to the subblock that follows with the comma delimiter
1131	          archiveprefix.or.eprinttype ":" * eprint * % subblock with eprinttype and eprint number
1132	        }
1133	      if$
1134	    }
1135	  if$
1136	}
1137	
1138	% urlbst...
1139	% Functions for making hypertext links.
1140	% In all cases, the stack has (link-text href-url)
1141	%
1142	% make 'null' specials
1143	FUNCTION {make.href.null}
1144	{
1145	  pop$
1146	}
1147	% make hypertex specials
1148	FUNCTION {make.href.hypertex}
1149	{
1150	  "\special {html:&lt;a href=" quote$ *
1151	  swap$ * quote$ * "> }" * swap$ *
1152	  "\special {html:&lt;/a>}" *
1153	}
1154	% make hyperref specials
1155	FUNCTION {make.href.hyperref}
1156	{
1157	  "\href {" swap$ * "} {\path{" * swap$ * "}}" *
1158	}
1159	FUNCTION {make.href}
1160	{ hrefform #2 =
1161	    'make.href.hyperref      % hrefform = 2
1162	    { hrefform #1 =
1163	        'make.href.hypertex  % hrefform = 1
1164	        'make.href.null      % hrefform = 0 (or anything else)
1165	      if$
1166	    }
1167	  if$
1168	}
1169	
1170	% If inlinelinks is true, then format.url should be a no-op, since it's
1171	% (a) redundant, and (b) could end up as a link-within-a-link.
1172	FUNCTION {format.url}
1173	{ inlinelinks #1 = url empty$ or
1174	   { "" }
1175	   { hrefform #1 =
1176	       { % special case -- add HyperTeX specials
1177	         urlintro "\url{" url * "}" * url make.href.hypertex * }
1178	       { urlintro "\url{" * url * "}" * }
1179	     if$
1180	   }
1181	  if$
1182	}
1183	FUNCTION {format.eprint}
1184	{ eprint empty$
1185	    { "" }
1186	    { eprintprefix eprint * eprinturl eprint * make.href }
1187	  if$
1188	}
1189	
1190	FUNCTION {format.doi}
1191	{ doi empty$
1192	    { "" }
1193	    { doi parse.doi % leaves "https://doi.org/DOI" DOI on the stack
1194	      's := 't :=
1195	      doiform #1 =
1196	        { "\doi{" s * "}" * }
1197	        { doiprefix s * t make.href }
1198	      if$
1199	    }
1200	  if$
1201	}
1202	
1203	FUNCTION {format.pubmed}
1204	{ pubmed empty$
1205	    { "" }
1206	    { pubmedprefix pubmed * pubmedurl pubmed * make.href }
1207	  if$
1208	}
1209	
1210	% Output a URL.  We can't use the more normal idiom (something like
1211	% `format.url output'), because the `inbrackets' within
1212	% format.lastchecked applies to everything between calls to `output',
1213	% so that `format.url format.lastchecked * output' ends up with both
1214	% the URL and the lastchecked in brackets.
1215	FUNCTION {output.url}
1216	{ url empty$
1217	    'skip$
1218	    { new.block
1219	      format.url output
1220	      format.lastchecked output
1221	    }
1222	  if$
1223	}
1224	
1225	FUNCTION {output.web.refs}
1226	{
1227	  new.block
1228	  inlinelinks
1229	    'skip$ % links were inline -- don't repeat them
1230	    { % If the generated DOI will be the same as the URL,
1231	      % then don't print the URL (thanks to Joseph Wright
1232	      % for (the original version of) this code,
1233	      % at http://tex.stackexchange.com/questions/5660)
1234	      adddoi
1235	          doi empty$ { "X" } { doi parse.doi pop$ } if$ % DOI URL to be generated
1236	          url empty$ { "Y" } { url } if$          % the URL, or "Y" if empty
1237	          =                                       % are the strings equal?
1238	          and
1239	        'skip$
1240	        { output.url }
1241	      if$
1242	      addeprints eprint empty$ not and
1243	        { format.eprint output.nonnull }
1244	        'skip$
1245	      if$
1246	      adddoi doi empty$ not and
1247	        { format.doi output.nonnull }
1248	        'skip$
1249	      if$
1250	      addpubmed pubmed empty$ not and
1251	        { format.pubmed output.nonnull }
1252	        'skip$
1253	      if$

1285	% Webpage entry type.
1286	% Title and url fields required;
1287	% author, note, year, month, and lastchecked fields optional
1288	% See references
1289	%   ISO 690-2 http://www.nlc-bnc.ca/iso/tc46sc9/standard/690-2e.htm
1290	%   http://www.classroom.net/classroom/CitingNetResources.html
1291	%   http://neal.ctstateu.edu/history/cite.html
1292	%   http://www.cas.usf.edu/english/walker/mla.html
1293	% for citation formats for web pages.
1294	FUNCTION {webpage}
1295	{ output.bibitem
1296	  author empty$
1297	    { editor empty$
1298	        'skip$  % author and editor both optional
1299	        { format.editors output.nonnull }
1300	      if$
1301	    }
1302	    { editor empty$
1303	        { format.authors output.nonnull }
1304	        { "can't use both author and editor fields in " cite$ * warning$ }
1305	      if$
1306	    }
1307	  if$
1308	  new.block
1309	  title empty$ 'skip$ 'possibly.setup.inlinelink if$
1310	  format.title "title" output.check
1311	  inbrackets onlinestring output
1312	  new.block
1313	  year empty$
1314	    'skip$
1315	    { format.date "year" output.check }
1316	  if$
1317	  % We don't need to output the URL details ('lastchecked' and 'url'),
1318	  % because fin.entry does that for us, using output.web.refs.  The only
1319	  % reason we would want to put them here is if we were to decide that
1320	  % they should go in front of the rather miscellaneous information in 'note'.
1321	  new.block
1322	  note output
1323	  fin.entry
1324	}

1853	{ #0 int.to.chr$ 'last.label :=
1854	  "" 'next.extra :=
1855	  #0 'last.extra.num :=

1857	  last.extra.num.blank 'last.extra.num.extended :=
1858	  #0 'number.label :=

1860	FUNCTION {forward.pass}
1861	{ last.label label =
1862	    { last.extra.num #1 + 'last.extra.num :=
1863	      last.extra.num "z" chr.to.int$ >
1864	       { "a" chr.to.int$ 'last.extra.num :=
1865	         last.extra.num.extended #1 + 'last.extra.num.extended :=
1866	       }
1867	       'skip$
1868	      if$
1869	      last.extra.num.extended last.extra.num.blank >
1870	        { last.extra.num.extended int.to.chr$
1871	          last.extra.num int.to.chr$
1872	          * 'extra.label := }
1873	        { last.extra.num int.to.chr$ 'extra.label := }
1874	      if$
1875	    }
1876	    { "a" chr.to.int$ 'last.extra.num :=
1877	      "" 'extra.label :=
1878	      label 'last.label :=
1879	    }
1880	  if$
1881	  number.label #1 + 'number.label :=
1882	}
1883	FUNCTION {reverse.pass}
1884	{ next.extra "b" =
1885	    { "a" 'extra.label := }
1886	    'skip$
1887	  if$
1888	  extra.label 'next.extra :=
1889	  extra.label
1890	  duplicate$ empty$
1891	    'skip$
1892	    { year field.or.null #-1 #1 substring$ chr.to.int$ #65 < 
1893	      { "{\natexlab{" swap$ * "}}" * }
1894	      { "{(\natexlab{" swap$ * "})}" * }
1895	    if$ }
1896	  if$
1897	  'extra.label :=
1898	  label extra.label * 'label :=

      "content": "%%% Modification of BibTeX style file acl_natbib_nourl.bst\n%%% ... by urlbst, version 0.9.1 (marked with \"% urlbst\")\n%%% See &lt;https://purl.org/nxg/dist/urlbst> and repository &lt;https://heptapod.host/nxg/urlbst>\n%%% Modifications Copyright 2002–23, Norman Gray,\n%%% and distributed under the terms of the LPPL; see README for discussion.\n%%%\n%%% Added webpage entry type, and url and lastchecked fields.\n%%% Added eprint support.\n%%% Added DOI support.\n%%% Added PUBMED support.\n%%% Added hyperref support.\n%%% Original headers follow...\n\n%%\n%% This is file `acl_natbib_basic.bst',\n%% generated with the docstrip utility.\n%%\n%% The original source files were:\n%%\n%% merlin.mbs  (with options: `ay,nat,pres,ed-au,keyxyr,blkyear,dt-beg,yr-per,note-yr,num-xser,pre-edn,xedn,nfss')\n%% ----------------------------------------\n%% *** Intended for ACL conferences ***\n%% \n%% Copyright 1994-2011 Patrick W Daly\n % ===============================================================\n % IMPORTANT NOTICE:\n % This bibliographic style (bst) file has been generated from one or\n % more master bibliographic style (mbs) files, listed above.\n %\n % This generated file can be redistributed and/or modified under the terms\n % of the LaTeX Project Public License Distributed from CTAN\n % archives in directory macros/latex/base/lppl.txt; either\n % version 1 of the License, or any later version.\n % ===============================================================\n % Name and version information of the main mbs file:\n % \\ProvidesFile{merlin.mbs}[2011/11/18 4.33 (PWD, AO, DPC)]\n %   For use with BibTeX version 0.99a or later\n %-------------------------------------------------------------------\n % This bibliography style file is intended for texts in ENGLISH\n % This is an author-year citation style bibliography. As such, it is\n % non-standard LaTeX, and requires a special package file to function properly.\n % Such a package is    natbib.sty   by Patrick W. Daly\n % The form of the \\bibitem entries is\n %   \\bibitem[Jones et al.(1990)]{key}...\n %   \\bibitem[Jones et al.(1990)Jones, Baker, and Smith]{key}...\n % The essential feature is that the label (the part in brackets) consists\n % of the author names, as they should appear in the citation, with the year\n % in parentheses following. There must be no space before the opening\n % parenthesis!\n % With natbib v5.3, a full list of authors may also follow the year.\n % In natbib.sty, it is possible to define the type of enclosures that is\n % really wanted (brackets or parentheses), but in either case, there must\n % be parentheses in the label.\n % The \\cite command functions as follows:\n %   \\citet{key} ==>>                Jones et al. (1990)\n %   \\citet*{key} ==>>               Jones, Baker, and Smith (1990)\n %   \\citep{key} ==>>                (Jones et al., 1990)\n %   \\citep*{key} ==>>               (Jones, Baker, and Smith, 1990)\n %   \\citep[chap. 2]{key} ==>>       (Jones et al., 1990, chap. 2)\n %   \\citep[e.g.][]{key} ==>>        (e.g. Jones et al., 1990)\n %   \\citep[e.g.][p. 32]{key} ==>>   (e.g. Jones et al., 1990, p. 32)\n %   \\citeauthor{key} ==>>           Jones et al.\n %   \\citeauthor*{key} ==>>          Jones, Baker, and Smith\n %   \\citeyear{key} ==>>             1990\n %---------------------------------------------------------------------\n\n%% 2025 modified to truncate author lists of more than 20 authors\n\nENTRY\n  { address\n    archivePrefix\n    author\n    booktitle\n    chapter\n    edition\n    editor\n    eid\n    eprint\n    eprinttype % = archivePrefix\n    howpublished\n    institution\n    journal\n    key\n    month\n    note\n    number\n    organization\n    pages\n    publisher\n    school\n    series\n    title\n    type\n    volume\n    year\n    doi % urlbst\n    pubmed % urlbst\n    url % urlbst\n    lastchecked % urlbst\n  }\n  {}\n  { label extra.label sort.label short.list }\nINTEGERS { output.state before.all mid.sentence after.sentence after.block }\n% urlbst...\n% urlbst constants and state variables\nSTRINGS { urlintro\n  eprinturl eprintprefix doiprefix doiurl pubmedprefix pubmedurl\n  citedstring onlinestring linktextstring\n  openinlinelink closeinlinelink }\nINTEGERS { hrefform doiform inlinelinks makeinlinelink\n  addeprints adddoi addpubmed }\nFUNCTION {init.urlbst.variables}\n{\n  % The following constants may be adjusted by hand, if desired\n\n  % The first set allow you to enable or disable certain functionality.\n  #1 'addeprints :=\t% 0=no eprints; 1=include eprints\n  #2 'hrefform :=\t% 0=no crossrefs; 1=hypertex hrefs; 2=hyperref hrefs\n  #1 'inlinelinks :=\t% 0=URLs explicit; 1=URLs attached to titles\n  #1 'adddoi :=\t% 0=no DOI resolver; 1=include it\n  #1 'addpubmed :=\t% 0=no PUBMED resolver; 1=include it\n  #0 'doiform :=\t% 0=with href; 1=with \\doi{}\n\n  % String constants, which you _might_ want to tweak.\n  \"online\" 'onlinestring :=\t% label that a resource is online\n  \"[link]\" 'linktextstring :=\t% anonymous link text\n  \"http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/\" 'pubmedurl :=\t% prefix to make URL from PUBMED\n  \"https://doi.org/\" 'doiurl :=\t% prefix to make URL from DOI\n  \"doi:\" 'doiprefix :=\t% printed text to introduce DOI\n  \"https://arxiv.org/abs/\" 'eprinturl :=\t% prefix to make URL from eprint ref\n  \"cited \" 'citedstring :=\t% label in \"lastchecked\" remark\n  \"arXiv:\" 'eprintprefix :=\t% text prefix printed before eprint ref\n  \"PMID:\" 'pubmedprefix :=\t% text prefix printed before PUBMED ref\n  \"URL: \" 'urlintro :=\t% text prefix before URL\n\n  % The following are internal state variables, not configuration constants,\n  % so they shouldn't be fiddled with.\n  #0 'makeinlinelink :=     % state variable managed by possibly.setup.inlinelink\n  \"\" 'openinlinelink :=     % ditto\n  \"\" 'closeinlinelink :=    % ditto\n}\nINTEGERS {\n  bracket.state\n  outside.brackets\n  open.brackets\n  within.brackets\n  close.brackets\n}\n% ...urlbst to here\nFUNCTION {init.state.consts}\n{ #0 'outside.brackets := % urlbst...\n  #1 'open.brackets :=\n  #2 'within.brackets :=\n  #3 'close.brackets := % ...urlbst to here\n\n  #0 'before.all :=\n  #1 'mid.sentence :=\n  #2 'after.sentence :=\n  #3 'after.block :=\n}\nSTRINGS { s t}\n% urlbst\nFUNCTION {output.nonnull.original}\n{ 's :=\n  output.state mid.sentence =\n    { \", \" * write$ }\n    { output.state after.block =\n        { add.period$ write$\n          newline$\n          \"\\newblock \" write$\n        }\n        { output.state before.all =\n            'write$\n            { add.period$ \" \" * write$ }\n          if$\n        }\n      if$\n      mid.sentence 'output.state :=\n    }\n  if$\n  s\n}\n\n% urlbst...\n% Minimal DOI parsing.\n% Given a DOI on the stack, check whether it starts with 'doiurl' or not.\n% In either case, leave on the stack first a DOI with, and then a DOI without, the URL prefix.\nFUNCTION {parse.doi}\n{\n  #1 doiurl text.length$ substring$\n  doiurl =\n    { doi\n      doi doiurl text.length$ #1 + #999 substring$ }\n    { doiurl doi *\n      doi }\n  if$\n}\n% The following three functions are for handling inlinelink.  They wrap\n% a block of text which is potentially output with write$ by multiple\n% other functions, so we don't know the content a priori.\n% They communicate between each other using the variables makeinlinelink\n% (which is true if a link should be made), and closeinlinelink (which holds\n% the string which should close any current link.  They can be called\n% at any time, but start.inlinelink will be a no-op unless something has\n% previously set makeinlinelink true, and the two ...end.inlinelink functions\n% will only do their stuff if start.inlinelink has previously set\n% closeinlinelink to be non-empty.\n% (thanks to 'ijvm' for suggested code here)\nFUNCTION {uand}\n{ 'skip$ { pop$ #0 } if$ } % 'and' (which isn't defined at this point in the file)\nFUNCTION {possibly.setup.inlinelink}\n{ makeinlinelink hrefform #0 > uand\n    { doi empty$ adddoi uand\n        { pubmed empty$ addpubmed uand\n            { eprint empty$ addeprints uand\n                { url empty$\n                    { \"\" }\n                    { url }\n                  if$ }\n                { eprinturl eprint * }\n              if$ }\n            { pubmedurl pubmed * }\n          if$ }\n%        { doiurl doi * }\n        { doi empty$\n            { \"XXX\" }\n            { doi parse.doi pop$ }\n          if$\n        }\n      if$\n      % an appropriately-formatted URL is now on the stack\n      hrefform #1 = % hypertex\n        { \"\\special {html:&lt;a href=\" quote$ * swap$ * quote$ * \"> }{\" * 'openinlinelink :=\n          \"\\special {html:&lt;/a>}\" 'closeinlinelink := }\n        { \"\\href {\" swap$ * \"} {\" * 'openinlinelink := % hrefform=#2 -- hyperref\n          % the space between \"} {\" matters: a URL of just the right length can cause \"\\% newline em\"\n          \"}\" 'closeinlinelink := }\n      if$\n      #0 'makeinlinelink :=\n      }\n    'skip$\n  if$ % makeinlinelink\n}\nFUNCTION {add.inlinelink}\n{ openinlinelink empty$\n    'skip$\n    { openinlinelink swap$ * closeinlinelink *\n      \"\" 'openinlinelink :=\n      }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {output.nonnull}\n{ % Save the thing we've been asked to output\n  's :=\n  % If the bracket-state is close.brackets, then add a close-bracket to\n  % what is currently at the top of the stack, and set bracket.state\n  % to outside.brackets\n  bracket.state close.brackets =\n    { \"]\" *\n      outside.brackets 'bracket.state :=\n    }\n    'skip$\n  if$\n  bracket.state outside.brackets =\n    { % We're outside all brackets -- this is the normal situation.\n      % Write out what's currently at the top of the stack, using the\n      % original output.nonnull function.\n      s\n      add.inlinelink\n      output.nonnull.original % invoke the original output.nonnull\n    }\n    { % Still in brackets.  Add open-bracket or (continuation) comma, add the\n      % new text (in s) to the top of the stack, and move to the close-brackets\n      % state, ready for next time (unless inbrackets resets it).  If we come\n      % into this branch, then output.state is carefully undisturbed.\n      bracket.state open.brackets =\n        { \" [\" * }\n        { \", \" * } % bracket.state will be within.brackets\n      if$\n      s *\n      close.brackets 'bracket.state :=\n    }\n  if$\n}\n\n% Call this function just before adding something which should be presented in\n% brackets.  bracket.state is handled specially within output.nonnull.\nFUNCTION {inbrackets}\n{ bracket.state close.brackets =\n    { within.brackets 'bracket.state := } % reset the state: not open nor closed\n    { open.brackets 'bracket.state := }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {format.lastchecked}\n{ lastchecked empty$\n    { \"\" }\n    { inbrackets citedstring lastchecked * }\n  if$\n}\n% ...urlbst to here\nFUNCTION {output}\n{ duplicate$ empty$\n    'pop$\n    'output.nonnull\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {output.check}\n{ 't :=\n  duplicate$ empty$\n    { pop$ \"empty \" t * \" in \" * cite$ * warning$ }\n    'output.nonnull\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {fin.entry.original} % urlbst (renamed from fin.entry, so it can be wrapped below)\n{ add.period$\n  write$\n  newline$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {new.block}\n{ output.state before.all =\n    'skip$\n    { after.block 'output.state := }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {new.sentence}\n{ output.state after.block =\n    'skip$\n    { output.state before.all =\n        'skip$\n        { after.sentence 'output.state := }\n      if$\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {add.blank}\n{  \" \" * before.all 'output.state :=\n}\n\nFUNCTION {date.block}\n{\n  new.block\n}\n\nFUNCTION {not}\n{   { #0 }\n    { #1 }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {and}\n{   'skip$\n    { pop$ #0 }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {or}\n{   { pop$ #1 }\n    'skip$\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {new.block.checkb}\n{ empty$\n  swap$ empty$\n  and\n    'skip$\n    'new.block\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {field.or.null}\n{ duplicate$ empty$\n    { pop$ \"\" }\n    'skip$\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {emphasize}\n{ duplicate$ empty$\n    { pop$ \"\" }\n    { \"\\emph{\" swap$ * \"}\" * }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {tie.or.space.prefix} % puts ~ before the preceding part if it is of length <3\n{ duplicate$ text.length$ #3 <\n    { \"~\" }\n    { \" \" }\n  if$\n  swap$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {capitalize}\n{ \"u\" change.case$ \"t\" change.case$ }\n\nFUNCTION {space.word}\n{ \" \" swap$ * \" \" * }\n % Here are the language-specific definitions for explicit words.\n % Each function has a name bbl.xxx where xxx is the English word.\n % The language selected here is ENGLISH\nFUNCTION {bbl.and}\n{ \"and\"}\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.etal}\n{ \"et~al.\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.editors}\n{ \"editors\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.editor}\n{ \"editor\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.edby}\n{ \"edited by\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.edition}\n{ \"edition\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.volume}\n{ \"volume\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.of}\n{ \"of\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.number}\n{ \"number\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.nr}\n{ \"no.\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.in}\n{ \"in\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.pages}\n{ \"pages\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.page}\n{ \"page\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.chapter}\n{ \"chapter\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.techrep}\n{ \"Technical Report\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.mthesis}\n{ \"Master's thesis\" }\n\nFUNCTION {bbl.phdthesis}\n{ \"Ph.D. thesis\" }\n\nMACRO {jan} {\"January\"}\n\nMACRO {feb} {\"February\"}\n\nMACRO {mar} {\"March\"}\n\nMACRO {apr} {\"April\"}\n\nMACRO {may} {\"May\"}\n\nMACRO {jun} {\"June\"}\n\nMACRO {jul} {\"July\"}\n\nMACRO {aug} {\"August\"}\n\nMACRO {sep} {\"September\"}\n\nMACRO {oct} {\"October\"}\n\nMACRO {nov} {\"November\"}\n\nMACRO {dec} {\"December\"}\n\nMACRO {acmcs} {\"ACM Computing Surveys\"}\n\nMACRO {acta} {\"Acta Informatica\"}\n\nMACRO {cacm} {\"Communications of the ACM\"}\n\nMACRO {ibmjrd} {\"IBM Journal of Research and Development\"}\n\nMACRO {ibmsj} {\"IBM Systems Journal\"}\n\nMACRO {ieeese} {\"IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering\"}\n\nMACRO {ieeetc} {\"IEEE Transactions on Computers\"}\n\nMACRO {ieeetcad}\n {\"IEEE Transactions on Computer-Aided Design of Integrated Circuits\"}\n\nMACRO {ipl} {\"Information Processing Letters\"}\n\nMACRO {jacm} {\"Journal of the ACM\"}\n\nMACRO {jcss} {\"Journal of Computer and System Sciences\"}\n\nMACRO {scp} {\"Science of Computer Programming\"}\n\nMACRO {sicomp} {\"SIAM Journal on Computing\"}\n\nMACRO {tocs} {\"ACM Transactions on Computer Systems\"}\n\nMACRO {tods} {\"ACM Transactions on Database Systems\"}\n\nMACRO {tog} {\"ACM Transactions on Graphics\"}\n\nMACRO {toms} {\"ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software\"}\n\nMACRO {toois} {\"ACM Transactions on Office Information Systems\"}\n\nMACRO {toplas} {\"ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems\"}\n\nMACRO {tcs} {\"Theoretical Computer Science\"}\n\n% bibinfo.check avoids acting on missing fields while bibinfo.warn will\n% issue a warning message if a missing field is detected. Prior to calling\n% the bibinfo functions, the user should push the field value and then its\n% name string, in that order.\nFUNCTION {bibinfo.check}\n{ swap$\n  duplicate$ missing$\n    {\n      pop$ pop$\n      \"\"\n    }\n    { duplicate$ empty$\n        {\n          swap$ pop$\n        }\n        { swap$\n          pop$\n        }\n      if$\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {bibinfo.warn}\n{ swap$\n  duplicate$ missing$\n    {\n      swap$ \"missing \" swap$ * \" in \" * cite$ * warning$ pop$\n      \"\"\n    }\n    { duplicate$ empty$\n        {\n          swap$ \"empty \" swap$ * \" in \" * cite$ * warning$\n        }\n        { swap$\n          pop$\n        }\n      if$\n    }\n  if$\n}\nINTEGERS { nameptr namesleft numnames }\n\n\nSTRINGS  { bibinfo}\n\nFUNCTION {format.names}\n{ 'bibinfo :=\n  duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$ {\n  's :=\n  \"\" 't :=\n  #1 'nameptr :=\n  s num.names$ 'numnames :=\n  numnames 'namesleft :=\n    { namesleft #0 > }\n    { s nameptr\n      \"{ff~}{vv~}{ll}{, jj}\" % first name first for all authors\n      format.name$\n      bibinfo bibinfo.check\n      't :=\n      nameptr #1 >\n        {\n          nameptr #19\t% truncate after 19 names\n          #1 + =\n          numnames #20\t% if there are more than 20 names\n          > and\n            { \"others\" 't :=\n              #1 'namesleft := }\n            'skip$\n          if$\t\t% end truncation of long list of names\n          namesleft #1 >\n            { \", \" * t * }\n            {\n              s nameptr \"{ll}\" format.name$ duplicate$ \"others\" =\n                { 't := }\n                { pop$ }\n              if$\n              numnames #2 >\n                { \",\" * }\n                'skip$\n              if$\n              t \"others\" =\n                {\n\t\t  %%                 \" \" * bbl.etal *\n\t\t  % compute the number of remaining authors\n\t\t  \" and \" * numnames nameptr - #1 + int.to.str$ * \" others\" *\n                }\n                {\n                  bbl.and\n                  space.word * t *\n                }\n              if$\n            }\n          if$\n        }\n        't\n      if$\n      nameptr #1 + 'nameptr :=\n      namesleft #1 - 'namesleft :=\n    }\n  while$\n  } if$\n}\nFUNCTION {format.names.ed}\n{\n  format.names\n}\nFUNCTION {format.key}\n{ empty$\n    { key field.or.null }\n    { \"\" }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {format.authors}\n{ author \"author\" format.names\n}\nFUNCTION {get.bbl.editor}\n{ editor num.names$ #1 > 'bbl.editors 'bbl.editor if$ }\n\nFUNCTION {format.editors}\n{ editor \"editor\" format.names duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$\n    {\n      \",\" *\n      \" \" *\n      get.bbl.editor\n      *\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {format.note}\n{\n note empty$\n    { \"\" }\n    { note #1 #1 substring$\n      duplicate$ \"{\" =\n        'skip$\n        { output.state mid.sentence =\n          { \"l\" }\n          { \"u\" }\n        if$\n        change.case$\n        }\n      if$\n      note #2 global.max$ substring$ * \"note\" bibinfo.check\n    }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {format.title}\n{ title\n  duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$\n    { \"t\" change.case$ }\n  if$\n  \"title\" bibinfo.check\n}\nFUNCTION {format.full.names}\n{'s :=\n \"\" 't :=\n  #1 'nameptr :=\n  s num.names$ 'numnames :=\n  numnames 'namesleft :=\n    { namesleft #0 > }\n    { s nameptr\n      \"{vv~}{ll}\" format.name$\n      't :=\n      nameptr #1 >\n        {\n          namesleft #1 >\n            { \", \" * t * }\n            {\n              s nameptr \"{ll}\" format.name$ duplicate$ \"others\" =\n                { 't := }\n                { pop$ }\n              if$\n              t \"others\" =\n                {\n                  \" \" * bbl.etal *\n                }\n                {\n                  numnames #2 >\n                    { \",\" * }\n                    'skip$\n                  if$\n                  bbl.and\n                  space.word * t *\n                }\n              if$\n            }\n          if$\n        }\n        't\n      if$\n      nameptr #1 + 'nameptr :=\n      namesleft #1 - 'namesleft :=\n    }\n  while$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {author.editor.key.full}\n{ author empty$\n    { editor empty$\n        { key empty$\n            { cite$ #1 #3 substring$ }\n            'key\n          if$\n        }\n        { editor format.full.names }\n      if$\n    }\n    { author format.full.names }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {author.key.full}\n{ author empty$\n    { key empty$\n         { cite$ #1 #3 substring$ }\n          'key\n      if$\n    }\n    { author format.full.names }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {editor.key.full}\n{ editor empty$\n    { key empty$\n         { cite$ #1 #3 substring$ }\n          'key\n      if$\n    }\n    { editor format.full.names }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {make.full.names}\n{ type$ \"book\" =\n  type$ \"inbook\" =\n  or\n    'author.editor.key.full\n    { type$ \"proceedings\" =\n        'editor.key.full\n        'author.key.full\n      if$\n    }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {output.bibitem.original} % urlbst (renamed from output.bibitem, so it can be wrapped below)\n{ newline$\n  \"\\bibitem[{\" write$\n  label write$\n  \")\" make.full.names duplicate$ short.list =\n     { pop$ }\n     { * }\n   if$\n  \"}]{\" * write$\n  cite$ write$\n  \"}\" write$\n  newline$\n  \"\"\n  before.all 'output.state :=\n}\n\nFUNCTION {n.dashify}\n{\n  't :=\n  \"\"\n    { t empty$ not }\n    { t #1 #1 substring$ \"-\" =\n        { t #1 #2 substring$ \"--\" = not\n            { \"--\" *\n              t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=\n            }\n            {   { t #1 #1 substring$ \"-\" = }\n                { \"-\" *\n                  t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=\n                }\n              while$\n            }\n          if$\n        }\n        { t #1 #1 substring$ *\n          t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't :=\n        }\n      if$\n    }\n  while$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {word.in}\n{ bbl.in capitalize\n  \" \" * }\n\nFUNCTION {format.date}\n{ year \"year\" bibinfo.check duplicate$ empty$\n    {\n    }\n    'skip$\n  if$\n  extra.label *\n  before.all 'output.state :=\n  after.sentence 'output.state :=\n}\nFUNCTION {format.btitle}\n{ title \"title\" bibinfo.check\n  duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$\n    {\n      emphasize\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {either.or.check}\n{ empty$\n    'pop$\n    { \"can't use both \" swap$ * \" fields in \" * cite$ * warning$ }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {format.bvolume}\n{ volume empty$\n    { \"\" }\n    { bbl.volume volume tie.or.space.prefix\n      \"volume\" bibinfo.check * *\n      series \"series\" bibinfo.check\n      duplicate$ empty$ 'pop$\n        { swap$ bbl.of space.word * swap$\n          emphasize * }\n      if$\n      \"volume and number\" number either.or.check\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {format.number.series}\n{ volume empty$\n    { number empty$\n        { series field.or.null }\n        { series empty$\n            { number \"number\" bibinfo.check }\n            { output.state mid.sentence =\n                { bbl.number }\n                { bbl.number capitalize }\n              if$\n              number tie.or.space.prefix \"number\" bibinfo.check * *\n              bbl.in space.word *\n              series \"series\" bibinfo.check *\n            }\n          if$\n        }\n      if$\n    }\n    { \"\" }\n  if$\n}\n\nFUNCTION {format.edition}\n{ edition duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$\n    {\n      output.state mid.sentence =\n        { \"l\" }\n        { \"t\" }\n      if$ change.case$\n not\n      and\n    }\n    { t #1 #1 substring$\n      duplicate$ \"-\" =\n      swap$ duplicate$ \",\" =\n      swap$ \"+\" =\n      or or\n        { #1 'multiresult := }\n        { t #2 global.max$ substring$ 't := }\n      if$\n    }\n  while$\n  multiresult\n}\nFUNCTION {format.pages}\n{ pages duplicate$ empty$ 'skip$\n    { duplicate$ multi.page.check\n        {\n          bbl.pages swap$\n          n.dashify\n        }\n        {\n          bbl.page swap$\n        }\n      if$\n      tie.or.space.prefix\n      \"pages\" bibinfo.check\n      * *\n    }\n  if$\n}\nFUNCTION {
  "server": "gpu-6000pro-2",
  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/abstract.tex\n\\begin{abstract}\nAs language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification, constructing a 180K-passage benchmark spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 depths under controlled full-replacement recursive chains at the 1--1.5B scale.\nA Random Forest over 19 statistical features, dominated by the perplexity tail family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}), achieves 69--86\\% four-class accuracy (d1--d3 balanced: 59--82\\%; chance = 25\\%/33\\%); the convergence of RF, XGBoost, and LightGBM within 0.4\\,pp confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier choice.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% four-class (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, and a single-cell prompted-generation test 69.5\\%.\nCross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%, d1--d3: 45.9\\%) reveals that decoding strategies induce structurally distinct degradation trajectories, a finding we formalize via the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$ for all three families).\nPreliminary cross-scale experiments (7--14B, 54--70\\%) provide an existence proof that depth signal persists at larger scales, though confounded by five factors including quantization and vocabulary mismatch.\nBinary detectors and membership inference baselines (30--35\\%) fall ${>}$43\\,pp below our pipeline, confirming that ordinal depth estimation requires distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\\end{abstract}\nENDOFFILE"
}
```

{
  "server": "gpu-6000pro-2",
  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/theory.tex\n\\subsection{Theoretical Grounding}\n\\label{sec:method:theory}\n\nRecursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.\nThis tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed by its dominance across all 9 cells (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}).\nSurprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}\nTail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\\alpha \\in (0,1)$ \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.\nOur empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\\to$d4v5; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \\cite{bertrand2024_stability}.\n5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\\%, chance = 20\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}\nLarger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\\alpha$ closer to 1), so EDD should decrease with scale.\nQwen2.5-14B confirms this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B), while d1--d3 AUCs remain above 0.70 (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}\nThe contraction rate $\\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.\nTemperature scaling ($\\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.\nGPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).\nTop-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\\%) across the atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\nENDOFFILE"
}

{
  "server": "gpu-6000pro-2",
  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/discussion.tex\n\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThis paper formalizes recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification under controlled full-replacement chains.\nThe perplexity tail feature family dominates depth discrimination across all nine model--strategy cells; five features retain 97.6\\% of full performance, and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$ for all three families (confirmed by 5-class extension, 71--80\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:depth4_ext}).\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% four-class (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%; binary detectors and membership inference methods achieve only 30--35\\%, confirming that depth-specific distributional features are necessary.\n\n\\paragraph{Accumulation settings.}\nOur benchmark uses full-replacement chains where each depth fully replaces the training data.\nIn practice, model training increasingly involves accumulation: mixing human data with synthetic outputs at unknown proportions across iterations \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.\nUnder accumulation, the depth signal should attenuate because each generation retains a fraction of human-originated distributional properties, slowing tail erosion.\nOur full-replacement results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability; the 66.4\\% cross-model transfer and 59.1\\% cross-strategy transfer should be interpreted as ceiling estimates for the mixed-data regime.\nExtending the framework to accumulation settings, where depth becomes a continuous mixing parameter rather than a discrete label, is a priority for future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale and deployment implications.}\nThe depth signal attenuates with model scale: at 14B, d0/d1 separability drops sharply ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), though d1--d3 discrimination persists above chance.\nThis suggests that depth estimation complements rather than replaces binary detection for practical data auditing: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary where depth estimation is redundant ($>$97\\% d0 recall), while depth estimation provides the finer ordinal resolution that binary methods cannot.\nA practical deployment path proceeds from controlled settings, where model and strategy are known, to partially known settings via strategy-identification routing, to fully unknown settings requiring strategy-invariant features (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Open challenges.}\nCross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) remains the primary bottleneck, driven by structurally distinct feature-space trajectories across decoding strategies.\nDifferent deployment regimes may require adapted feature sets: tail statistics dominate at shallow depths while repetition features gain importance at deeper recursions (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}).\nScale-dependent signal attenuation, depth-quality entanglement, and extension to accumulation settings are open challenges.\nENDOFFILE"
}

{
  "server": "gpu-6000pro-2",
  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/introduction.tex\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nWe study this question under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nInstruction-tuned variants exhibit monotonically decreasing AUC (regularization), whereas base models can show non-monotonic trajectories; instruction tuning acts as a regularizer on the depth signal rather than a source of degradation.\n\nFollowing the tradition of benchmark-driven empirical studies in NLP, our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization rather than algorithmic novelty; the convergence of multiple classifiers (RF, XGBoost, LightGBM within 0.4\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce a lightweight pipeline: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and benchmark.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the EDD metric, and construct a 180K-sample benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family dominates depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$ for all three families, validated by 5-class (d0--d4) extension (71--80\\%); at $T{=}0.75$, Pythia achieves $p{=}0.001$ with graded confidence across families.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization and cross-strategy analysis.} Cross-model LOO 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, single-cell prompted 69.5\\%, preliminary cross-scale 7--14B (54--70\\%, attenuating with scale). We identify a structural barrier in cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%, d1--d3: 45.9\\%): decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories, with d1/d2 boundary inversion under strategy mismatch. Surface-text controls (zero-shot LLMs 30--36\\%) and binary detection/membership inference baselines (DetectGPT, Min-K\\%: 30--33\\%) confirm the signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\\end{itemize}\nENDOFFILE"
}

  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/related_work.tex\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}\nRecursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, an effect formalized as exponential tail decay via scaling laws \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails,seddik2024synthetic} and extended to autophagous loops in the MAD framework \\cite{alemohammad2024_mad}.\nSubsequent work refines the conditions under which collapse occurs: \\citet{bertrand2024_stability} identify convergence to a fixed point under mixed real-synthetic retraining (providing theoretical grounding for our EDD ceiling, \\S\\ref{sec:method:theory}), \\citet{shi2025_closer_look} reframe collapse as a generalization-to-memorization transition, and \\citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} extend guarantees to broader model classes.\nMitigation \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, data scaling under constraint \\cite{muennighoff2023scaling}, non-monotonic dynamics \\cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence analysis \\cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \\cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026} are active areas.\n\\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple phenomena and call for operationally precise metrics; we respond by operationalizing tail-erosion predictions into a deployable ordinal classifier, revealing strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.\nAll assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}\nThe detection literature \\cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, BiScope \\cite{guo2024_biscope}, GLTR \\cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \\cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \\cite{yang2024_dnagpt}), geometric approaches based on intrinsic dimensionality \\cite{tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \\cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.\nM4-SemEval \\cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, RAID \\cite{dugan2024_raid}, and MAGE \\cite{li2024_mage} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions.\n\\citet{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms} distinguish authorship attribution (which model?) from verification (human or AI?); depth estimation introduces a third axis: which recursive iteration.\nAll these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Membership Inference and Data Provenance.}\nMin-K\\% Prob \\cite{shi2024_minkprob} detects pretraining membership via low-probability token statistics, related to our perplexity features but limited to binary classification; Datamodels \\cite{ilyas2022datamodels} trace predictions to influential training examples; cryptographically undetectable watermarks \\cite{christ2024_undetectable} require generator cooperation.\nDepth estimation occupies a distinct niche: ordinal, retroactive, and cooperation-free.\n\n\\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \\emph{which} model generated text; \\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} characterize \\emph{how} text degrades under iterative generation without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.\nOur task performs the inverse: ordinal depth estimation from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}\nCORAL \\cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \\cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses; \\citet{kasa2024_ordinality} benchmark ordinal techniques for NLP; \\citet{janitza2016_ordinal_rf} show RF implicitly respects ordinal structure when inter-class distances are monotonic.\nWe adopt CORAL and CLOC as baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}\nDecoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.\nOur atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) shows these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation.\nENDOFFILE"
}

```json
{
  "server": "gpu-6000pro-2",
  "command": "cat << 'ENDOFFILE' > /root/autodl-tmp/recursive_gen_depth_est/paper/method.tex\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBOS-seeded generation may amplify depth signal; prompted-generation validation (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) confirms signal persistence ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model; this overlaps with one generator but confers no systematic advantage (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%).\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}; Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\\footnote{AUC profiles decay monotonically for 1--1.5B models (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}); Qwen2.5-7B shows non-monotonic patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), where the contiguous formulation is more conservative (Limitations).}\nWe report $T{=}0.70$ as primary threshold, corresponding to adequate discrimination in ROC analysis (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$); sensitivity at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV).\\footnote{EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees; the main 4-class RF uses 200 trees.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), QWK\\,=\\,0.89; CORAL ordinal regression matches within 0.6\\,pp ($p > 0.05$; \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nComparisons against CORAL \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, and other baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.\nENDOFFILE"
}

Real-world contamination typically involves accumulation, where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions across iterations \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}. Under accumulation, depth signal should attenuate because each generation retains human-originated distributional properties. Our results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability; extending the framework to accumulation settings, where depth becomes a continuous mixing parameter, is a priority for future work.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_depth}), as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled experimental setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--14B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show signal that attenuates with scale; at 14B, d0/d1 separability nearly vanishes ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$) while d1--d3 discrimination persists; this constitutes a boundary condition for the current feature set (confounded by five factors; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_confounds}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp); a two-stage pipeline is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\nIn strategy-unknown settings, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\%; the pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp, though gains vary across models (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$97\\%), inflating four-class accuracy.\nCross-model transfer of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\nWe report d1--d3 balanced accuracy alongside all four-class headlines to address this inflation.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nConfidence thresholding ($P(\\mathrm{d0}) \\geq 0.15$) reduces pooled FPR from 34\\%/17\\% to 2.8\\%/0.7\\% on C4/FineWeb at minimal accuracy cost ($-$0.3\\,pp); high FPR is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a fundamental calibration defect, though the optimal threshold remains domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nCross-seed replication covers 3 of 9 cells (Pythia, OLMo, GPT-2~XL nucleus); seed=43 yields $+$0.1 to $+$3.2\\,pp higher accuracy, with 2/3 deltas statistically significant (non-overlapping CIs).\nFeature rankings and depth ordering are consistent across seeds, but single-seed absolute accuracy carries ${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty; cross-cell comparisons should be interpreted cautiously.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nPrompted generation was validated on only 1 of 9 cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus); generalization to other model--strategy configurations under prompted conditions is not established.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.\nENDOFFILE"
